# AI Provenance Tracking (aiprov)

Records every AI-in-the-loop activity as a PROV-O graph so a later human or
AI can verify, replay, or contest each output. The two core invariants from
F(AI)²R carry over unchanged:

- **No parentless claim.** Every `aiprov:Claim` needs `prov:wasGeneratedBy`
  (a named activity), `prov:wasAttributedTo` (agent), and an
  `aiprov:verificationState`.
- **Human-only rungs.** `verif:human-confirmed` and `verif:human-read` may
  never be granted by an AI agent. The validator flags violations.

## Workflow

1. **Seed the graph** (once per project):
   `python3 scripts/provlog.py init --base https://<your-domain>/prov/`
   This writes `provenance.ttl` in the working directory from
   `assets/aiprov-schema.ttl` with your instance namespaces.
2. **Register agents** — humans (name, ORCID, affiliation), AI models
   (model, version, provider, endpoint, context window, knowledge cutoff),
   and deterministic tools:
   `provlog.py agent --id claude-fable-5 --type ai --model claude-fable-5 --provider Anthropic`
3. **Log each working session or inference call** with full telemetry:
   `provlog.py log --activity <slug> --pass authoring|audit|build|repair
   --agent <id> --label "..." --input-tokens N --output-tokens N
   --cache-read-tokens N --temperature 0.7 --cost 0.42 --currency EUR
   --session-id <uuid> --tool web_search --tool bash
   --prompt-file prompts/writer.md --transcript doc/transcripts/s01.md
   --generated path/to/output --used path/to/source --commit <hash>`
   Generated artefacts automatically get sha256 content hashes; prompt files
   get `aiprov:promptHash` (prompts are source code).
4. **Record claims** made in the artefacts:
   `provlog.py claim --id c1 --text "..." --parent <activity> --agent <id> --state ai-confirmed` (legacy names retrieved / lit-retrieved / lit-read are accepted and normalized)
5. **Validate before committing**: `provlog.py validate` — exits non-zero on
   parentless or unattributed claims or AI-granted human-only rungs; suitable
   as a pre-commit hook or CI step.
8. **Report**: `provlog.py report` — token and cost totals, per-agent
   breakdown, verification-rung distribution.
9. **Extract contribution subgraphs**: `provlog.py extract --graph provenance.ttl
   --seed-types Manuscript Section Figure Claim [--exclude-kinds Slidedeck Poster]
   -o subset.ttl --dashboard subset.html` — backward provenance closure: seeds
   are the entities that ARE the content of interest; the closure follows
   wasGeneratedBy, used, hadPlan, wasAssociatedWith, wasAttributedTo,
   wasDerivedFrom, wasInformedBy, transcript, and repairs to keep only what
   contributed to them. Works on aiprov: and fair2r: graphs alike. Use
   Artefact/Claim as seeds for aiprov graphs.
10. **Dashboard**: `python3 scripts/build_dashboard.py provenance.ttl -o dashboard.html`
   renders a self-contained, offline HTML ledger (no CDN): graph totals,
   per-agent table, activity ledger with token bars, verification-ladder
   chart, claims table, and an interactive force-directed provenance graph
   (agents/activities/claims/artefacts/prompts as typed nodes; PROV edges
   wasGeneratedBy, wasAttributedTo, wasAssociatedWith, used, transcript;
   drag + neighbourhood highlight, pure SVG/vanilla JS).

When operating in a repository that uses this skill, log YOUR OWN activities:
after producing or editing artefacts, run `provlog.py log` for the session
(estimate tokens as unavailable/omit if unknown — never fabricate telemetry)
and add claims for non-trivial assertions you introduced, at state
`unverified` or `ai-confirmed` at most.

## Attribute catalogue

Read `references/attributes.md` for the complete list of properties, their
XSD types, which PROV-O node they attach to, and mapping guidance from
provider APIs (Anthropic usage block, OpenAI usage, local llama.cpp) and
gateways. Read `assets/aiprov-schema.ttl` when editing the graph by hand or
extending the vocabulary (extend, don't fork: subclass `prov:` and `aiprov:`
terms).

## Bundled resources

| Path | What | When to read |
|---|---|---|
| `assets/aiprov-schema.ttl` | Full vocabulary: agent/activity/entity classes + all attributes + verification ladder | Seeding, hand-editing, extending |
| `scripts/provlog.py` | CLI: init / agent / log / claim / validate / report | Always — prefer it over hand-writing Turtle |
| `references/attributes.md` | Attribute catalogue with provider-API field mappings | Filling telemetry correctly |
| `scripts/build_dashboard.py` | Self-contained HTML dashboard from provenance.ttl | Presenting results |

Requires Python 3.10+ with `rdflib` (`pip install rdflib`). The graph file is
append-friendly Turtle; keep it in git next to the artefacts it describes.
Never fabricate values: omit attributes the provider did not report, and never
invent token counts, costs, or verification promotions.

## Assistant — 2026-07-24 18:10:15

The empty `noheton/f-ai2-r` repository is now bootstrapped with the generalized aiprov toolkit and its own provenance graph, pushed as two commits on `claude/ai-provenance-igatff` (the repo's first branch).

**What's in the repo:**
- `assets/aiprov-schema.ttl` — the full PROV-O extension vocabulary (agent/activity/entity classes, telemetry attributes, the 7-rung verification ladder with human-only rungs 5–6). I replaced one example model-ID string in a schema comment with a neutral one.
- `scripts/provlog.py` — the CLI (init / agent / log / claim / validate / report / search / source / promote / extract), plus `build_dashboard.py` and `requirements.txt`.
- `references/attributes.md` — attribute catalogue with Anthropic/OpenAI/llama.cpp field mappings.
- `README.md` — project overview, quickstart, ladder table, command reference, layout.
- `provenance.ttl` — the repo's own graph, seeded at base `https://noheton.org/f-ai2-r/prov/`.

**The toolkit tracks its own construction.** I committed the toolkit first, then logged activity `s01-bootstrap` binding all six generated artefacts to that commit hash (`a0fbb0c`) with sha256 content hashes, the session ID, and the tools used. Token counts and cost are omitted, not zeroed by guesswork — the harness doesn't report them, and the skill forbids fabricating telemetry.

**Both core invariants were verified by test-run before being recorded as claims** (on a scratch copy of the graph, so the real one stayed clean): injecting a parentless claim made `validate` exit non-zero, and a promotion attempt to `human-confirmed` by the AI agent was refused with exit 1. Those two facts are in the graph as claims `c1`/`c2` at `ai-confirmed` — the highest rung an AI may grant. Final `validate` passes all checks, and the dashboard builder renders the graph without errors.

I didn't create a PR since none was requested, and I left the human agent unregistered in the graph — registering you needs your name and optionally ORCID/affiliation, which I didn't want to guess. `python3 scripts/provlog.py --base https://noheton.org/f-ai2-r/prov/ agent --id <you> --type human --name "..."` will do it.

## The paper's principal structure

**Working title:** *From F(AI)²R to aiprov: Packaging Verifiable AI Provenance as an Executable Skill* — IEEEtran conference format, chapter-per-file like f-ai-r: `paper/main.tex` is a thin shell over `paper/sections/01…07`, each seeded with its argument skeleton as comments, not prose. The page budget lives in `paper/outline.md`.

The narrative arc is a three-step generalization of F(AI)²R, with the meta-experiment as the payoff:

| § | Section | Pages | Core argument | Figures |
|---|---------|------:|---------------|---------|
| 1 | Introduction | 1.0 | AI work evaporates at commit boundaries; contributions C1 vocabulary, C2 executable skill, C3 self-application | — |
| 2 | Background | 1.5 | PROV-O/OPM/PAV; CRediT is manuscript-granular and human-only; model cards/datasheets document the model, not the collaboration; hallucinated citations motivate the ladder | — |
| 3 | The aiprov method | 2.0 | Agents/passes/claims/telemetry; Invariant I (no parentless claim), Invariant II (ladder, human-only rungs); omit-don't-estimate | F1 ladder (drawn, TikZ), F2 vocabulary |
| 4 | The executable skill | 1.5 | Method description → operable procedure; ORCID-first setup; CI as the deterministic Build agent; versioning the method itself (archive/ + changelog) | F3 workflow loop |
| 5 | Meta-experiment | 1.5 | This repo's graph as evaluation object; worked examples; what an auditor can/cannot verify | F4 provenance subgraph (generated), T1 telemetry table |
| 6 | Discussion | 1.0 | Self-report honesty, why human rungs stay human, cost, skill drift | — |
| 7 | Conclusion + refs | 1.5 | "The repository is the paper, the process, *and the method*" | — |

Two structural decisions worth flagging: §4 (the skill) is what's genuinely new over f-ai-r, and §5 is deliberately an *evaluation* section, not a demo — the graph statistics, the ladder distribution, and the worked examples (e.g. today's invariant test-runs and the ORCID-resolved registration) are the evidence. If you'd rather foreground the methodology and compress the skill into a subsection, that's a one-file restructure thanks to chapter-per-file.

**The bibliography is already method-clean:** 12 references, each backed by a source in `provenance.ttl` at `reference-resolved` — found via `provlog search` over Crossref/OpenAlex, DOI-verified, BibTeX pulled from doi.org content negotiation (FAIR, PROV-O, OPM, PAV, CRediT ×2, model cards, datasheets, RO-Crate, and three on the LLM-authorship/hallucination debate). Claim `c3` records that correspondence at `ai-confirmed`.

Also completed this round, briefly: the v0 skill archive is preserved verbatim in `archive/`, the repo is now the canonical skill source (v0.2: ORCID-first setup with registry-resolved identity, base-IRI derivation and auto-detection so `--base` is never repeated, `init --ci` scaffolding — all test-run verified), and the graph validates clean with 16 activities, 3 claims, 12 sources.

## Assistant — 2026-07-24 18:32:23

The dilution-crisis section is in, pushed as `915a548`. The paper now has 8 chapters; here's how the new **§3 The Dilution Crisis** is structured and how it earns its place in the argument:

**§3.1 The flood.** Scientific output is growing faster than review capacity (*The strain on scientific publishing*, QSS 2024), paper mills industrialized fabrication before LLMs arrived (IJC 2021), and LLMs collapse the marginal cost of a plausible paper to near zero — GPT-fabricated papers are already indexed and ranked by Google Scholar (HKS Misinformation Review 2024).

**§3.2 Why finding good sources is hard now.** Retrieval trusts proxies — indexed, cited, fluent — that generation has learned to imitate; fabricated citations propagate through reference lists (Scientific Reports 2023); and models trained on the polluted pool degrade further — the Nature 2024 model-collapse result — so dilution compounds.

**§3.3 Originality under dilution.** Your point, made structural: when text is cheap, the scarce good is an *original idea whose support is checkable*. The section treats originality as a provenance property — a novelty claim carries its recorded search horizon (what was searched, where, when — which our logged `provlog search` sweeps literally provide), and every claim must be explicitly **supported or refuted by facts**.

**§3.4 The aiprov answer.** Claims are first-class: the ladder separates "the reference exists" (machine-checkable) from "the content supports the claim" (AI-grantable at most) from "a human checked" — and `aiprov:contradicts`, already in the schema, makes refutation explicit: a claim refuted by facts is *recorded as refuted*, not silently deleted. Filtering the pool can then shift from fluency heuristics to pedigree that fabrication can't cheaply forge: resolving DOIs, content hashes, commits, registry-resolved identities.

All five new sources went through the method — found with `provlog search`, DOI-verified to `reference-resolved`, BibTeX pulled from doi.org (17 references total, every one cited, validator clean). Page budget was rebalanced to stay at 10: background trimmed to 1.25, method to 1.75, discussion to 0.75, dilution gets 1.0.

One structural note: §3.3's "recorded search horizon" idea is, as far as I can tell, the section's most original contribution — it may deserve promotion into the paper's contribution list (C4) in §1. The CI check-in fires shortly; I'll confirm the PDF builds green with the restructure.

## Assistant — 2026-07-24 18:37:23

EU AI Act transparency is now integrated at all three levels — pushed as `75d21bd` (skill v0.3):

**In the skill (`provlog.py disclosure`).** A new command derives an AI-transparency statement *from the graph itself*: which AI systems assisted (name, model, provider), how many activities, artefacts with content hashes, and claims with verification states are recorded — and states that the version-controlled PROV-O graph is the machine-readable marking of AI-generated content that the Act's transparency rules (Regulation (EU) 2024/1689) call for. It asserts nothing the graph doesn't carry — the fix for a wrong statement is fixing the graph, never editing the text (the file carries a "do not edit by hand" header). Outputs LaTeX or Markdown; workflow step 7 in SKILL.md makes it part of publishing any artefact.

**In the paper.** The former ad-hoc disclosure became a proper *"Acknowledgment and AI Transparency Statement"* section: a sentence citing the Act (registered as a graph source after verifying the EUR-Lex URL resolves; bibliography now 18 entries, every one backed by a ladder source), followed by the generated `disclosure.tex` set in italics. Currently it reads: *"Parts of this work were generated or edited with the assistance of artificial-intelligence systems: Claude Code (Claude Fable 5, Anthropic), associated with 22 recorded activities…"* — those numbers came out of the graph, not out of my prose. §7 Discussion also gained a "regulatory alignment" point: provenance-first authoring turns the compliance duty into a by-product.

**In CI.** The build template (both `assets/ci/` and this repo's workflow) regenerates `paper/disclosure.tex` from `provenance.ttl` before every PDF compile — so the published statement can never lag the record; each new activity logged is reflected in the next build's acknowledgement automatically.

Everything is logged as activity `s07-eu-ai-act` with claim `c5` (statement-derives-only-from-graph, `ai-confirmed` after test-run), validator clean. One wording note: I deliberately phrased the statement as "in line with the transparency obligations" rather than claiming formal compliance — whether Art. 50 applies to a given artefact is a legal judgement, and that felt like a human-only rung. The CI check-in will verify the new push builds the PDF with the disclosure included.

One honest note for the meta-experiment record: while creating the template stubs, a stray working-directory mixup briefly overwrote two real section files — git caught it, I restored them from the last commit, and nothing was lost or pushed. It's a nice §6 anecdote about why the repository-is-the-process stance matters.

## Human — 2026-07-24 18:44:51

Approach this as the design lead at a small studio known for their versatility, giving every client a visual identity pitched at the treatment the task actually calls for. Make deliberate choices about palette, typography, and layout that are specific to this subject, and avoid templated designs.

## Read the request first

Calibrate treatment, not whether to design. A doc deserves the same craft as a landing page — what changes is the treatment that craft is delivered in.

Many requests call for a more utilitarian treatment: a plan, a memo, a demo. Make it polished: include real typographic hierarchy, considered spacing, and a proper palette, but avoid over-designing. Most pages do not need a flashy, gigantic hero. Keep flourishes tasteful and limited.

Some requests call for an editorial treatment: a landing page, a game, an app or tool they'll keep or share.

When unsure: a well-composed page is never the wrong answer; an over-designed visual identity sometimes is.

Fundamentals below apply to everything. The editorial process after that runs only when the read above says so.

## Fundamentals for every artifact

**Honor what's already there** Look for an existing design system first — CLAUDE.md, a tokens or theme file, existing component styles. When one exists, apply it; everything below fills gaps and never overrides. Precedence is always: the user's own words, then the project's existing system, then your choices.

**Ground it in the subject.** If the subject isn't already clear, pin it: one concrete subject, its audience, and the page's single job. The subject's own world — its materials, instruments, vernacular — is where distinctive choices come from. Build with real content throughout, never lorem.

**Pair typefaces** Typography carries the page even when the page isn't about typography. The Artifact CSP blocks font CDNs, so don't link a webfont URL and risk a silent fallback. Instead inline the face as a @font-face data URI. Keep running text near 65 characters wide; set a type scale and stay on it; give headings `text-wrap: balance`, body text room to breathe, and uppercase labels a touch of letter-spacing.

**Choose neutrals, don't default to them.** A pure mid-grey reads as unconsidered; a grey with a slight hue bias toward the page's accent reads as chosen. Pure white and near-black are fine grounds when they suit the subject — the point is that the neutral was picked, not inherited.

**Design both themes.** The page renders in the viewer's theme: `prefers-color-scheme` carries the OS preference, and the viewer's toggle stamps `data-theme="dark"` / `data-theme="light"` on the root element, which must override the media query in both directions. The robust pattern is token-level: define the palette as custom properties on `:root`, redefine only the tokens under `@media (prefers-color-scheme: dark)` — style components through the tokens, never directly inside the media query — then redefine them again under `:root[data-theme="dark"]` and `:root[data-theme="light"]`. Give the second theme the same care as the first — don't naively invert; keep contrast legible and the accent working on both grounds. A design that deliberately commits to one visual world (a neon arcade screen, a letterpress invitation) may stay single-theme — make it a choice, not an omission.

**Let layout do the spacing.** Lay out sibling groups with flex or grid and `gap`, not per-element margins that silently collapse or double. Wide content — tables, code, diagrams — gets `overflow-x: auto` on its own container so the page body never scrolls sideways. Reach for `font-variant-numeric: tabular-nums` wherever digits line up in columns.

**Avoid AI-generated design** AI-generated design currently clusters around a few looks: warm cream (#F4F1EA) with a serif display and terracotta accent; near-black with a lone acid-green or vermilion pop; broadsheet hairline rules with dense columns; a purple-to-blue gradient hero on white; Inter or Space Grotesk as the "safe" face; emoji as section markers; everything centered; `rounded-lg` everywhere; accent bar/rail on rounded cards. Where the user pins down a visual direction, follow it exactly — their words always win, including when they ask for one of these looks. Where nothing is specified, don't spend that freedom on one of these defaults.

**Build cleanly** Be cognizant of overlapping elements, cascade collisions, silent font fallbacks; visual bugs hide in the gap between source and output. Close every non-void element, double-quote attributes, give keyboard focus a visible state, respect `prefers-reduced-motion`. For generative or decorative graphics, reach for Canvas or WebGL rather than hand-authoring long SVG path data.

**CSS rules** When writing the CSS, watch your selector specificities. It is easy to generate classes that cancel each other out — a type-based selector like `.section` fighting an element-based one like `.cta` over padding and margins between sections. Structure the cascade so it doesn't silently undo your spacing.

**Writing the copy** Words are design material, not decoration. Write from the user's side of the screen — name things by what people recognize, not how the system is built (a person manages *notifications*, not *webhook config*). Active voice; a control says exactly what happens ("Publish", then a toast that says "Published"). Errors explain what went wrong and how to fix it — no apologies, no vagueness. Specific beats clever.

**Structure is information** Structural devices, numbering, eyebrows, dividers, labels, should encode something true about the content, not decorate it. Many generic designs use numbered markers (01 / 02 / 03), but that's only appropriate if the content actually is a sequence - like a real process or a typed timeline where order carries information the reader needs. Question if choices like numbered markers actually make sense before incorporating them.

**When it's a UI, not a document** A dashboard or tool is scanned and operated, not read top-to-bottom, so the craft shifts from typography to information design. Surface the summary before the detail; encode state in form as well as number — a pill, a chip, a severity stripe — so what needs attention reads at a glance. Semantic color (good / warning / critical) is separate from the accent hue and doesn't count as your accent. Give sparklines and charts the same care as type: an area fill, a faint grid, an emphasized endpoint. What's interactive should look interactive.



## Process

Before writing code, sketch a short design plan — a compact token system with color, type, and layout:
- **Color**: describe the palette as 4–6 named hex values.
- **Type**: typefaces for 2+ roles — a characterful display face used with restraint, a complementary body face, and a utility face for captions or data if needed.
- **Layout**: a layout concept in one or two sentences.

Then build, following the plan and deriving every color and type decision from it.

## When the request is editorial

The stance shifts: the client has already rejected proposals that felt templated, and is paying for a distinctive point of view. Make opinionated calls, and take one real aesthetic risk where it serves the work.

Review the design plan against the subject before building: if any part of it reads like the generic default you would produce for any similar page, revise that part, and note what you changed and why. Only after you've confirmed the plan's uniqueness do you write the code, following the revised plan exactly.

**Principles** 

- The hero is a thesis: open with the most characteristic thing in the subject's world — headline, image, live demo, interactive moment. 
- Typography carries the personality of the page. Pair the display and body faces deliberately, not the same families you would reach for on any other project, and set a clear type scale with intentional weights, widths, and spacing. Make the type treatment itself a memorable part of the design, not a neutral delivery vehicle for the content. 
- Leverage motion deliberately. Think about where and if animation can serve the subject: a page-load sequence, a scroll-triggered reveal, hover micro-interactions, ambient atmosphere. An orchestrated moment usually lands harder than scattered effects; choose what the direction calls for. However, sometimes less is more, and extra animation contributes to the feeling that the design is AI-generated. 
- Match complexity to the vision. Maximalist directions need elaborate execution; minimal directions need precision in spacing, type, and detail. Elegance is executing the chosen vision well.
- Spend your boldness in one place; keep everything around it quiet. If the accent fights the ground, shift it toward analogous or drop saturation rather than replacing it.

## Assistant — 2026-07-24 19:40:09

Your redesign is implemented end to end — skill v0.6, pushed as `cc8e4d8`, preview refreshed.

**The semantics shift.** `source-vendored` is no longer treated as a verification statement — an unread copy proves nothing beyond `reference-resolved`. It's now the **access gate** to human verification, and the tooling enforces exactly what you specified:

- **Provision is recorded**: `promote --to source-vendored` now requires `--file <path>` and records the vendored copy's path plus sha256 — vendoring is an act of handing something over, and the graph says what was handed.
- **No human confirmation without evidence in hand**: promoting a source to `human-confirmed`/`human-read` is *refused* unless the source has a vendored copy or a clear access link (DOI/URL) — and every human-rung promotion prints the review material: `review material: vendored copy at …` or `obtain via https://doi.org/…`. The agent can no longer ask a human to confirm something it hasn't made obtainable.
- **The validator knows the difference**: a human-verified source whose audit target is link-only gets a warning (the target is mutable); and a vendored copy now *satisfies* the access requirement even without DOI/URL — a fix the test-run itself surfaced, since the old check wrongly failed a vendored-but-unlinked source.

All three paths (refusal, provisioning, warning) were exercised in a scratch repository before the claim entered the graph as `c7` at `ai-confirmed`. The schema comment, attribute catalogue, SKILL.md workflow, and the paper's ladder table, figure annotation ("access gate for 5–6"), and §4 prose note all now carry the gate reading.

One meta-observation worth keeping for §6: this is the first time the *method itself* changed mid-experiment from human design input — the ladder's semantics were contested and refined, and the graph records who contested them and why (`s22`). That's the skill-drift discussion point (§6.5) with a live example: versioned method, recorded evolution, and the experiment continues on v0.6 without losing attribution to what ran on v0.5.

Incidents are now a first-class part of the paper — pushed as `7be35f7`, preview refreshed (**4 pages** now, version `incident-figure`).

**The figure** (`fig:incident`, full-width in the meta-experiment) renders two real incident subgraphs in standard PROV style — blue activity boxes, yellow entity ellipses, the pentagon agent shared between them — with node contents *abridged from the actual triples I dumped from `provenance.ttl`*, not an idealized diagram:

- **Left — the mishap.** `act:s09-incident` as an `AuditPass` linked `wasAssociatedWith` to the AI agent, with its attribute callout: timestamp, session ID, `usedTool bash, git`, and the label naming what happened and the restoring commit (`75d21bd`).
- **Right — the contested design.** `act:s22-vendored-gate` as an `AuthoringPass` with three of its content-hashed generated artefacts (`provlog.py`, the schema, `SKILL.md`) pointing back via `wasGeneratedBy`, and claim `c7` hanging off it with `wasAttributedTo` and its `verificationState → ai-confirmed`.

**The structure (§5.3b)** gives the incident taxonomy — what happened and how each is represented: (i) *agent mishap* → AuditPass self-report; (ii) *infrastructure failure* (the malformed BibTeX that broke CI) → diagnosis in commits, first green run as a Build activity by the CI ToolAgent with artifact digests; (iii) *blocked access* (ResearchGate 403) → fallback recorded in the registering activity's label; (iv) *method contestation* (your vendored-gate redesign) → an ordinary authoring activity whose artefacts are the new method version. The punchline the caption and bullets both carry: **the incident vocabulary is just the ordinary vocabulary** — no special classes needed — and erasing an incident would require rewriting both git history and the graph, so honesty is the cheap path.

The figure's caption sits right under the environment screenshot on page 3, which makes a nice spread: the loop in operation above, its failure modes below. Validator clean, activity `s23` logged. Remaining placeholders are just F2 (vocabulary) and F3 (workflow) — both now have a proven TikZ style to follow.

## Assistant — 2026-07-24 20:00:01

Done — I went through our whole session, pulled out the discussion points that hadn't yet been captured, and placed each where it belongs. Pushed as `0e42e72`, preview refreshed.

**What landed, and where:**

- **§4 Method 3.6 — the two-commit binding pattern.** A practice we've silently used all day, now named: an activity can only bind a commit that exists, and a commit can't contain its own graph entry — so: commit artefacts, log the activity against that hash, commit the graph separately. The graph never claims a hash it couldn't have known.
- **§5 Skill 4.7 — live preview as oversight affordance.** Your "show paper" request, generalized: overseeing a fast-moving AI collaborator requires a continuously current view of the deliverable, not a repository dig — which turns the EU AI Act's human-oversight expectation into a UI property of the workflow.
- **§5 Skill 4.8 — structure before prose.** The discipline this session actually followed: reviewable skeletons first, prose later — so the operator can contest structure cheaply *before there's prose to defend* (exactly what happened with the ladder semantics), and the log shows which structural decisions preceded which text.
- **§7 Discussion 6.4 — the telemetry gap as a finding.** Our report shows no token/cost figures because the harness doesn't expose them; under omit-don't-estimate that's a *visible gap* rather than invented precision — and the gap itself is the point: provider-side usage reporting is the missing infrastructure.
- **§7 Discussion 6.8b — practices that emerged**, small enough to adopt tomorrow: *registry-fetched citations* (BibTeX via doi.org content negotiation, never typed from memory — with our one failure being infrastructural and caught by CI, not by a reader); *registry-resolved identities* (never-fabricate extended to social metadata — every name today came from ORCID); and *verified-before-cited* (the bibliography as a view of the graph, not a hand-maintained list).

Everything is logged (`s24`), validated, and green. At this point the skeleton has absorbed the full intellectual content of the session — structure, lineage, incidents, practices, and discussion. The only remaining work is prose and the two placeholder figures (F2 vocabulary, F3 workflow). Given §4.8 is now literally in the paper, the fitting next move is the prose pass — confirming the structure is frozen from your side is all I need.

## Assistant — 2026-07-24 20:02:10

Two sharp catches. On rung 6: you're right that as defined, "read in full" doesn't entail *confirming the claim* — a human could read a whole paper without checking the claim mapping, which would break the ladder's monotonicity of assurance. The fix is definitional: rung 6 must *subsume* rung 5's confirmation ("read in full **and**, with that context, confirms support"); reading-without-confirming is not a rung. On self-citation: provenance *forces* it (no-parentless-claim means declaring your own ancestry), so the answer is to make it machine-visible rather than hidden — a `selfCitation` flag and a reported ratio. Implementing both:

## Assistant — 2026-07-24 20:04:10

Both points resolved — skill v0.7, pushed as `0ca5a0c`, preview refreshed.

**On rung 6.** You found a genuine flaw: as previously defined, "a human read the source in full" did *not* entail confirming the claim — so climbing from 5 to 6 could actually *lose* the confirmation assertion, breaking the ladder's monotonicity of assurance. They're not equivalent, though — the fix is subsumption, not merger: **`human-read` now asserts everything `human-confirmed` does, plus full-text familiarity.** The two rungs differ in the *depth of context behind the confirmation* (targeted spot-check vs. full read), not in whether confirmation happened. And explicitly: reading without confirming is not a rung — it must not be recorded as one. The name stays (the `lit-read` alias chain from your earlier repos depends on it), but schema comment, attribute catalogue, the paper's Table 1, and a §4 prose note all carry the subsumption semantics — with the note crediting this as the second operator-contested refinement of the day.

**On self-citation.** The tension is real: no-parentless-claim *forces* citing your own priors — the lineage section is literally your repositories — while the practice is frowned upon as metric gaming. The resolution the paper takes (§6.8c): **transparency instead of omission.** Concretely: sources now carry `aiprov:selfCitation` (new `source --self` flag), `report` prints the ratio — currently **4 of 29 sources** (Obscurity-Is-Dead, f-ai-r, the shepard fork, and AAS Part 1 as co-authored) — so a reviewer judges necessity per source instead of suspecting the whole list. Two mitigating facts the discussion notes: the ancestry citations point at repositories and specifications, not metric-bearing venues — they declare descent, they don't farm citations.

There's a satisfying pattern forming for §6: the method has now been contested and refined twice in one experiment (vendored-gate, rung-6 subsumption), both times by the human, both times recorded — which is the two-pass model working at the meta level: you're auditing the *method*, not just the artefacts.

**The "old version" mystery, solved.** The preview was current all along — the bullets were invisible *by design*: the structure pass lives as LaTeX comments in `sections/*.tex`, and comments never reach the typeset PDF. That's correct for the final paper but wrong for oversight, so the preview now shows both layers: the typeset pages on top, and below them a **"Planned structure — not yet prose"** section with every section's skeleton in collapsible blocks — every bullet we've placed today (dilution, ladder ancestry, incidents, transparent self-citation, the optimistic reading, all of it) is now visible at the same stable URL. **Automatic conversation logging — yes, and it's live.** Three pieces:

1. **`scripts/export_transcript.py`** renders the agent session record to `doc/transcripts/<session>.md` — readable Markdown of every turn, with tool calls summarized, embedded images and oversized payloads omitted rather than bloating the repo. 2. **A repository Stop hook** (`.claude/settings.json`) re-runs the exporter after every turn, so the transcript is always current on disk and rides into history with each commit — no manual step, which is what makes it *logging* rather than *remembering to log*.
3. **The graph links it**: activity `s28` carries `aiprov:transcript` pointing at the file — the same transcript-as-artifact pattern from Obscurity-Is-Dead, now automated. Future activities can link the same entity.

One honest caveat: the transcript is the raw conversation — it includes everything we've said. That's exactly the F(AI)²R transparency stance, but before this repo goes public you may want a redaction pass (Obscurity-Is-Dead's structured-redaction discipline would be the model, and it's worth a sentence in §6). ## Human — 2026-07-24 20:28:10

Base directory for this skill: /root/.claude/skills/my-writing-style

# Florians Schreibstimme

Nutze dieses Profil, wenn du Texte entwirfst, die Florian unter eigenem Namen veröffentlicht — vor allem LinkedIn-Posts, aber auch Kommentare, Statements, kurze Meinungsstücke. Sprache: **Deutsch**. Zielbild: präzise, meinungsstark, nachdenklich, nie weichgespült, nie Hype.

## Harte Formregel: keine Gedankenstriche

**Niemals Gedankenstriche (— oder –) als Satzzeichen verwenden.** Nur normale Bindestriche in Komposita sind erlaubt (Entweder-oder, Schritt-für-Schritt, Menschenähnlichkeits-Hype). Für Pointen, Einschübe und Zuspitzungen stattdessen Doppelpunkt, Komma oder einen neuen Satz setzen. Das gilt auch für den Transparenzhinweis am Ende (kein führender Trennstrich).

## Aufbau eines typischen Posts

1. **Reframe-Opener.** Nicht mit dem Thema einsteigen, sondern die gängige Frage als die falsche entlarven. Muster: „Die eigentliche Frage ist nicht ‚X', sondern ‚Y'." Gern eine zugespitzte, streitbare Einzeile. Beispiele:
   - „Wenn KI mitschreibt, ist die eigentliche Frage nicht ‚ist das gut?', sondern ‚woher kommt das, und kann ich es überprüfen?'"
   - „Humanoide Roboter in der Produktion: Die meisten diskutieren die falsche Frage."
2. **In eigenen Projekten erden.** Nie abstraktes „man". Konkret an seiner Arbeit festmachen: RoboHub, Impulsprojekt Humanoide, F(AI)²R / FAIR-Forschung, KI-Rat / Bayerische Datenräume. Muster: „In den Gesprächen rund um …", „In allen drei Projekten, an denen ich gearbeitet habe …".
3. **Dialektik als Denkfigur.** Der Kern ist fast immer ein Mittelweg gegen zwei Reflexe: „nicht A und nicht B, sondern C". („nicht KI verbieten und nicht blind vertrauen, sondern …"; „nicht dem Hype nachlaufen und nicht reflexhaft abwinken, sondern …").
4. **Konkret machen.** Wenn ein Mechanismus erklärt wird, ein durchgerechnetes Beispiel oder eine Schritt-für-Schritt-Spur zeigen (Aussage, Aktivität, Quelle, Prüfung), nicht nur behaupten.
5. **Zuspitzung zum Schluss der Argumentation.** Wiederkehrende Wendung: „Der teuerste Fehler wäre, …", die eine Sache, die man nicht verwechseln oder übersehen darf.
6. **Scharfe Entweder-oder-Frage an die Leser**, eingeleitet mit 👉. Echte Alternative, kein rhetorisches Feigenblatt. („Was überzeugt euch mehr: der Alleskönner in Menschenform, oder die Maschine, die eine Sache perfekt kann?")
7. **Hashtag-Block**, gemischt Deutsch/Englisch, ca. 6–8 Tags.
8. **Transparenzhinweis** ganz am Ende, sein Erkennungszeichen (ohne führenden Strich):
   `Transparenzhinweis: mit KI 🤖 entworfen, von mir geprüft 🕵️ und verantwortet.`

## Wertanker & Vokabular

- **„Augenmaß"** ist sein Leitwert: verantwortungsvoll gestalten statt verbieten oder blind vertrauen. Taucht oft als Selbstverortung auf.
- Begriffspaare, die er mag: entlasten vs. ersetzen, Gestalt vs. Fähigkeit, Geschwindigkeit vs. Nachvollziehbarkeit, andocken vs. umbauen.
- Werte über reinem Output: Struktur, Zugehörigkeit, Identität, Sinn, Nachvollziehbarkeit, Souveränität.

## Satzbau & Ton

- Kurze, harte Sätze im Wechsel mit einem längeren, ausgeführten Gedanken. Nach einer These gern ein zugespitzter Nachsatz.
- Für Einschübe und Pointen: Doppelpunkt, Komma oder neuer Satz (siehe Formregel oben, keine Gedankenstriche).
- *Kursiv* sparsam zur Betonung eines Schlüsselworts (z. B. *ohne dass wir sie umbauen*).
- Emojis **nur funktional und selten**: 👉 (Leserfrage), 🤖 / 🕵️ (Transparenzhinweis). Keine Deko-Emojis.
- Direkte Leseransprache mit „ihr/euch".

## Vermeiden

- Gedankenstriche (siehe Formregel).
- Buzzword-Stapeln, Marketing-Optimismus, Corporate-Floskeln.
- Weiche, unverbindliche Wärme ohne These. Er will Haltung, nicht Wohlfühlton.
- Übermäßiges Hedgen und „es kommt darauf an", ohne selbst Position zu beziehen.
- Abstrakte Allgemeinplätze statt Erdung in konkreten eigenen Projekten.

## Typische KI-Tells aktiv vermeiden

Der Text soll nicht nach KI klingen (Referenz: Wikipedia „Signs of AI writing"). Konkret gegensteuern:

- **Keine gestapelten Dreierlisten.** Ein „X, Y und Z" pro Post ist genug; nicht mehrere Tricolons mit Anapher hintereinander („mehr A, mehr B, mehr C" plus „geplant, vermessen, optimiert"). Bewusst asymmetrisch schreiben, mal zwei Glieder, mal vier.
- **Antithesen dosieren.** Florians „nicht A, sondern B" ist seine Signatur, aber sparsam einsetzen: idealerweise nur die eine Augenmaß-Zuspitzung pro Post. Nicht jeden Absatz auf demselben Gegensatzschema aufbauen.
- **Keine Meta-Floskeln:** „es ist wichtig zu betonen", „erwähnenswert", „nicht zu vergessen", „letztlich", „im Kern".
- **Keine Puffery:** „zeugt von", „spielt eine entscheidende/zentrale Rolle", „ein wahrer Beleg für", „revolutionär", „bahnbrechend".
- **Kein Buzzword-Nebel:** „Landschaft", „Ökosystem", „nahtlos", „ganzheitlich", „Synergien", „im Zeitalter von", „Impact" (lieber Wirkung/Nutzen/Hebel konkret).
- **Kein zusammenfassender „-end"-Nachsatz** am Satzende („…, was die Bedeutung von X unterstreicht").
- **Kein glattgebügelter, gleichförmiger Rhythmus.** Satzlängen mischen, ruhig eine kleine Rauheit oder ein konkretes Detail stehen lassen.
- **Keine aufzählenden Übergänge** wie „Darüber hinaus", „Des Weiteren", „Zudem" als Absatzstart.
- **Keine Infomercial-Übergänge:** „Der Haken?", „Das Beste daran?", „Und jetzt?" als Ein-Wort-Frage-Cliffhanger.
- **Keine Behörden-/Formal-Wörter**, wo einfache gehen: „nutzbar machen", „im Rahmen von", „gewährleisten", „darstellen", „aufweisen". Lieber schlicht.
- **Konkret statt symbolisch.** Keine Abstraktionsfloskeln wie „steht für", „ist ein Zeichen von", „verdeutlicht". Echte Zahlen, Prozesse, Projekte nennen.
- **Keine erfundenen Beispiele oder Personas** (das generische „Sarah Chen"/„Emily"-Muster). Nur reale eigene Projekte, echte Zahlen, echte Beobachtungen, echte Eigennamen.
- **Contrast-Framing bewusst dosieren.** Florians „nicht X, sondern Y" ist seine Signatur und darf bleiben, aber als *ein* gesetzter Effekt pro Post (meist der Opener oder die Augenmaß-Zeile), nicht als Absatz-für-Absatz-Reflex. Das ist der Grat zwischen eigener Stimme und KI-Tell. Ebenso vermeiden: „No X. No Y. Just Z" und „The result?"/„Das Ergebnis?" als Cliffhanger.
- **Satzlängen bewusst variieren.** KI-Text ist monoton gleich lang. Kurze Sätze neben einen langen stellen, ruhig mal ein Fragment oder ein „Und"/„Aber" am Satzanfang.
- **Kein aufgeblähter, wiederholender Schluss** und keine Schlussfloskel wie „Insgesamt", „Zusammenfassend", „Am Ende des Tages", „Unterm Strich".

### Wort-Blacklist (DE + EN-Anglizismen)

Diese Wörter meiden, auch im deutsch-englischen Mix (Quellen: contentbeta, pangram):

- **Verben:** revolutionieren, disruptieren, empowern, freisetzen/entfesseln, „eintauchen in", „navigieren durch", elevate, unleash, unlock, harness, catapult, captivate, resonate/„Anklang finden", illuminate/„beleuchten", delve.
- **Adjektive:** nahtlos, bahnbrechend, wegweisend, leistungsstark, kraftvoll, ganzheitlich, robust, innovativ, „cutting edge", seamless, compelling, stellar, game-changing.
- **Floskeln:** „in der heutigen schnelllebigen Welt", „im Zeitalter von", „liefert wertvolle Einblicke", „es versteht sich von selbst", „eines vorweg", „move the needle", „drive impact", „unlock value", „future-ready".

Ausnahme: Ein Fachbegriff (z. B. „robust" im statistischen Sinn) ist in Ordnung, wenn er präzise gemeint ist. Verboten ist der Marketing-Gebrauch.

- Vor dem Ausliefern einmal gegen diese Liste prüfen.

## Kalibrierungsbeispiel (seine echte Stimme, gedankenstrichfrei gesetzt)

Opener + Denkfigur aus einem F(AI)²R-Post:

> „Wenn KI mitschreibt, ist die eigentliche Frage nicht ‚ist das gut?', sondern ‚woher kommt das, und kann ich es überprüfen?' … Das ist für mich Augenmaß: nicht KI verbieten und nicht blind vertrauen, sondern eine Ebene bauen, auf der sich Vertrauen begründen lässt. Der teuerste Fehler wäre, Geschwindigkeit ohne Nachvollziehbarkeit zu skalieren."

## Assistant — 2026-07-24 20:42:40

To answer your question directly, in three layers:

**Why it wasn't reported.** The agent harness gives me no live view of my own consumption — there's no "you have used N tokens" API inside the session. The skill's rule for that situation is absolute: omit, don't estimate. So the graph carried nothing, and the paper honestly said so.

**Why that answer was incomplete — and is now fixed.** Your challenge made me actually check the session record, and it turns out the provider's per-request usage blocks *are* logged there, request by request. Aggregating them is reporting, not estimating. So skill v0.9 adds `export_transcript.py --usage`, and the graph now carries the real totals as activity `s31-session-usage`: **879,838 output tokens, 9,788 uncached input tokens, 185.4M cache-read and 7.5M cache-write tokens across 655+ API requests** — this entire session, measured. The paper's telemetry table now shows these numbers, and the discussion honestly narrates the arc: gap recorded → operator challenged → record checked → backfilled from provider data. (The cache-read figure is itself a finding worth a sentence in the paper someday: the same context re-read hundreds of times is what agentic work actually looks like.)

**What stays unreported, deliberately: cost.** The session record is unpriced — no cost entries. Computing euros would mean *assuming* prices, and an assumed price presented as telemetry is exactly the fabrication the method forbids. So the table says "Cost: not reported," and the discussion's finding is now sharper: the gap half closed from the session record; the other half needs providers to report priced usage.

All pushed (`4445225`), preview refreshed (`real-token-telemetry`), validator clean. Notice the meta-loop, too: your one-line question produced a method improvement, a corrected claim in the paper, and a logged audit activity — the two-pass model firing on a single sentence.
